Horne,     Environmental Design, University of California, Berkeley, Berkeley, CA, United States, 6Department of Wetland Ecology, Institute The University of Melbourne, Australia     of Geography and Geoecology, Karlsruhe Institute of Technology, Rastatt, Germany, 7Department of Conservation Biology and Reviewed by:        Social-Ecological Systems, Helmholtz Centre for Environmental Research (UFZ), Leipzig, Germany, 8Department of Civil and Martin Thoms,       Environmental Engineering, University of California Davis, Davis, CA, United States, 9Center for Watershed Sciences, University of University of New England, Australia      California Davis, Davis, CA, United States, 10World Wildlife Fund, Washington, DC, United States, 11California Department of Rui Manuel Vitor Cortes,     Water Resources, Sacramento, CA, United States, 12American Rivers, Washington, DC, United States, 13River Partners, University of Trás-os-Montes and Alto      Sacramento, CA, United States, 14Department of Environmental Science, Policy and Management, University of California, Douro, Portugal      Berkeley, Berkeley, CA, United States, 15Environmental Science Associates, Sacramento, CA, United States, 16The Woods Institute for the Environment, Stanford University, Stanford, CA, United States *Correspondence: Anna Serra-Llobet [EMAIL_REDACTED]       Conventional ﬂood control has emphasized structural measures such as levees, reservoirs, and engineered channels—measures that typically simplify river channels and cut them off Specialty section: This article was submitted to     from their ﬂoodplain, both with adverse environmental consequences. Structural measures Freshwater Science,     tend to be rigid and not easily adapted to increased ﬂooding regimes resulting from a section of the journal Frontiers in Environmental Science environmental change. Such actions also limit the natural hydrologic beneﬁts of ﬂoodplains Received: 17 September 2021 such as storing ﬂoodwaters, improving water quality, providing habitat for invertebrates and Accepted: 31 December 2021           ﬁsh during periods of inundation, and supporting a multitude of cultural services. As these Published: 16 March 2022 beneﬁts are more widely recognized, policies are being adopted to encourage projects that Citation: reduce ﬂood risks and restore ﬂoodplain ecosystems, while acknowledging the social- Serra-Llobet A, Jähnig SC, Geist J, Kondolf GM, Damm C, Scholz M,           ecological context. The number of such projects, however, remains small. We assessed Lund J, Opperman JJ, Yarnell SM,         four multi-beneﬁt ﬂoodplain projects (two in California, United States, and two in Germany) and Pawley A, Shader E, Cain J, Zingraff-Hamed A, Grantham TE, characterized their drivers, history, and measures implemented. In both United States cases, Eisenstein W and Schmitt R (2022)        the dominant driver behind the project was ﬂood risk reduction, and ecosystem restoration Restoring Rivers and Floodplains for followed, in one case inadvertently, in the other as a requirement to receive a subsidy for a ﬂood Habitat and Flood Risk Reduction: Experiences in Multi-Beneﬁt Floodplain     risk reduction project. One German case was motivated by ecosystem restoration, but it was Management From California         more widely accepted because it also offered ﬂood management beneﬁts. The fourth case and Germany. Front. Environ. Sci. 9:778568. was conceived in terms of balanced goals of ﬂood risk reduction, ecosystem restoration, and doi: 10.3389/fenvs.2021.778568         recreation.
